<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:skew}
Let $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] The field $\operatorname{Div}A\in L^2(\Omega)^n$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{divDiv_zero}
 \operatorname{div}(\operatorname{Div}A)=0,\qquad \|\operatorname{Div}A\|_0\le\sqrt{n-1}\,|A|_1 ,
\end{equation}
where the first identity holds in the sense of distributions.
\item[(b)] Let $S\subset\Omega$ be a smooth hypersurface with unit normal ${\bf n}$, and let $A$ be piecewise $H^2$ on the two
sides of $S$ in a neighborhood of $S$. Then
on $S$, with one-sided traces $v^\pm$ and $[\![ v]\!]:=v^+-v^-$, where ${\bf n}$
points into the $+$ side (this convention is used throughout),
\begin{equation}\label{Div_jump}
 [\![ \operatorname{grad} A_{ij}]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A_{ij}]\!]\,{\bf n},\qquad [\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]\,{\bf n},\qquad {\bf n}\cdot[\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=0 .
\end{equation}
\item[(c)] For every $\mathbf j\in\mathbb{R}^n$ with $\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n}=0$, $K:=\mathbf j{\bf n}^T-{\bf n}\mathbf j^T\in\mathbb K_n$ satisfies $K{\bf n}=\mathbf j$; thus
$K'\mapsto K'{\bf n}$ maps $\mathbb K_n$ onto ${\bf n}^\perp:=\{\mathbf v\in\mathbb{R}^n:\mathbf v\cdot{\bf n}=0\}$. Its kernel
consists of the skew-symmetric maps of ${\bf n}^\perp$ (extended by
$K'{\bf n}=0$) and has dimension $(n-1)(n-2)/2$; it is trivial for $n=2$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
(a) For $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\Omega)$, $\langle\operatorname{div}\operatorname{Div}A,\phi\rangle=\sum_{i,j}\int_\Omega A_{ij}\,\partial_i\partial_j\phi\,dx=0$ by antisymmetry. Since $A_{ii}=0$, $(\operatorname{Div}A)_i$ has $n-1$ terms, and pointwise $|\operatorname{Div}A|^2\le(n-1)\sum_i\sum_{j\ne i}|\partial_jA_{ij}|^2=(n-1)\sum_{i<j}(|\partial_ja_{ij}|^2+|\partial_ia_{ij}|^2)\le(n-1)\sum_{i<j}|\operatorname{grad} a_{ij}|^2$. (b) Each $A_{ij}\in H^1(\Omega)$ has a single-valued
trace on $S$, so its tangential derivatives agree from the two sides and the jump of its gradient is normal. Hence $[\![ (\operatorname{Div}A)_i]\!]=\sum_j[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A_{ij}]\!]n_j$, and ${\bf n}\cdot[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]{\bf n}=0$ because $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]$ is
skew. (c) $K{\bf n}=\mathbf j\,({\bf n}\cdot{\bf n})-{\bf n}\,(\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n})=\mathbf j$. If $K'\in\mathbb K_n$ and $K'{\bf n}=0$, then ${\bf n}^TK'=-(K'{\bf n})^T=0$, so $K'$ maps ${\bf n}^\perp$ into itself and vanishes on ${\bf n}$; conversely, every skew-symmetric
map of ${\bf n}^\perp$, extended by $0$ on ${\bf n}$, is in $\mathbb K_n$. The skew-symmetric maps of the $(n-1)$-dimensional space ${\bf n}^\perp$ form a space of dimension $(n-1)(n-2)/2$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:forms}
Identify $A$ with the $2$-form $\sum_{i<j}A_{ij}\,dx_i\wedge dx_j$. Then $d(\star A)=(\operatorname{Div}A)\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$: the $(n-1)$-form $d(\star A)$ corresponds to $\operatorname{Div}A$ in the identification $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)\simeq H\Lambda^{n-1}(\Omega)$, ${\bf v}\mapsto{\bf v}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$. Equivalently, $\operatorname{Div}A$ is the vector field of the $1$-form $d^*A$, where $d^*$ is the formal $L^2$-adjoint of $d$ (the codifferential): for every compactly supported $1$-form $\alpha=\sum_i\alpha_i\,dx_i$, $\int_\Omega\sum_{i<j}(\partial_i\alpha_j-\partial_j\alpha_i)A_{ij}\,dx=\int_\Omega\boldsymbol\alpha\cdot\operatorname{Div}A\,dx$ after integration by parts. The same operator is called $\mathrm{curl}$ in \cite[Section~2.4]{ChenJiYuZhang2026}. We keep the notation $\operatorname{Div}$, which shows that the operator is the row-wise divergence of a skew-symmetric matrix and that $\operatorname{div}\operatorname{Div}A=0$.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:Rd}
For $q\in L^2(\Omega)$, $\mathcal{R}_nq\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{Rd_bound}
 \operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=q,\qquad \|\mathcal{R}_nq\|_0\le\frac{2C_{x_0}}{n}\|q\|_0,\qquad
 \|\mathcal{R}_nq\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\Bigl(1+\frac{4C_{x_0}^2}{n^2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\|q\|_0 .
\end{equation}
Moreover, \eqref{Rd} defines $\mathcal{R}_nq$ on all of $\mathbb{R}^n$, with $\mathcal{R}_nq\in L^2_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^n)^n$ and $\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=\widetilde q$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
\emph{Bound.} For $g\in L^2(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $t\in(0,1]$ the substitution $y=x_0+t(x-x_0)$, $dx=t^{-n}dy$, gives $\|g(x_0+t(\cdot-x_0))\|_{0,\Omega}\le t^{-n/2}\|g\|_{0,\mathbb{R}^n}$, and Minkowski's integral inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{G_bound}
 \|G_g\|_{0,\Omega}\le\int_0^1t^{n-1}\|g(x_0+t(\cdot-x_0))\|_{0,\Omega}\,dt\le\|g\|_{0,\mathbb{R}^n}\int_0^1t^{\,n-1-n/2}\,dt
 =\frac2n\|g\|_{0,\mathbb{R}^n}.
\end{equation}
Since $|x-x_0|\le C_{x_0}$ on $\Omega$, $\|(x-x_0)G_g\|_{0,\Omega}\le\frac{2C_{x_0}}{n}\|g\|_{0,\mathbb{R}^n}$; for $g=\widetilde
q$ this is the second bound, since $\|\widetilde q\|_{0,\mathbb{R}^n}=\|q\|_{0,\Omega}$.

\emph{Identity.} For $g\in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n)$, since $(x-x_0)\cdot\operatorname{grad}_x\bigl[g(x_0+t(x-x_0))\bigr]=t\,\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}g(x_0+t(x-x_0))$, integration by parts in $t$ gives
\[
 (x-x_0)\cdot\operatorname{grad} G_g(x)=\int_0^1t^{n}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}g\bigl(x_0+t(x-x_0)\bigr)\,dt=g(x)-n\,G_g(x),
\]
hence $\operatorname{div}\bigl((x-x_0)G_g\bigr)=n\,G_g+(x-x_0)\cdot\operatorname{grad} G_g=g$. For $q\in L^2(\Omega)$ take $g_k\in
C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $g_k\to\widetilde q$ in $L^2(\mathbb{R}^n)$. The bound,
applied to $g_k-\widetilde q$, gives $(x-x_0)G_{g_k}\to\mathcal{R}_nq$ in $L^2(\Omega)$, so for $\phi\in
C_c^\infty(\Omega)$ $-\int_\Omega\mathcal{R}_nq\cdot\operatorname{grad}\phi=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_\Omega g_k\,\phi=\int_\Omega q\,\phi$, that is, $\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=q$ in
$\Omega$. The third bound follows from the first
two. The bound and the limit hold with
$\Omega$ replaced by any ball $B$ about $x_0$
(and $C_{x_0}$ by its radius), and $\phi\in C_c^\infty(B)$ gives
$-\int_B\mathcal{R}_nq\cdot\operatorname{grad}\phi=\int_B\widetilde q\,\phi$, that is, $\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=\widetilde q$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:poincare}
Formula \eqref{Rd} is the Poincar\'e homotopy operator with a fixed center in top degree \cite{Cartan1967}, written for vector
fields: for the $n$-form $u=\widetilde q\,dx_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_n$ it is the vector proxy of $\int_0^1t^{n-1}(x-x_0)\lrcorner\,u(x_0+t(x-x_0))\,dt$, and $\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=q$ is the top-degree homotopy relation.
Costabel and McIntosh \cite{CostabelMcIntosh2010} average such fixed-center path integrals over the center with a
smooth weight \cite[(3.9)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} to obtain operators with Sobolev mapping properties. Their \cite[(1.4)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} is the familiar three-dimensional analogue for $\operatorname{curl}$.
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:Rcont}
Let $S_q\subset\overline\Omega$ be closed, let $q\in L^\infty(\Omega)$ be continuous on
$\Omega\setminus S_q$, and let $S$ be a closed
set containing $\partial\Omega\cup S_q$, for instance $S=\partial\Omega\cup S_q$; then
$\widetilde q$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^n\setminus S$. Assume that
for every $x\in\overline\Omega\setminus\{x_0\}$ the open segment $\{x_0+t(x-x_0):0<t<1\}$ meets $S$
in a set of one-dimensional measure zero, for
instance in finitely many points. Then $(x-x_0)G_{\widetilde q}$
is continuous on $\overline\Omega$; in particular $\mathcal{R}_nq$ has no jumps.
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $x_k\to x$ in $\overline\Omega$. If $x=x_0$, then $|(x_k-x_0)G_{\widetilde q}(x_k)|\le\frac1n|x_k-x_0|\,\|q\|_{L^\infty}\to0$. If $x\ne x_0$, then $x_0+t(x-x_0)\notin S$ for almost every $t\in(0,1)$, and for such $t$, $\widetilde
q(x_0+t(x_k-x_0))\to\widetilde q(x_0+t(x-x_0))$ by the continuity of $\widetilde q$ on $\mathbb{R}^n\setminus S$; the integrand of $G_{\widetilde q}$ is bounded by $t^{n-1}\|q\|_{L^\infty}$, and dominated convergence gives $G_{\widetilde q}(x_k)\to G_{\widetilde q}(x)$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:R_fluxes}
For $q\in L^2(\Omega)$, $F_i(\mathcal{R}_nq)=0$ for $i=1,\ldots,b$, and $\langle\mathcal{R}_nq\cdot{\bf n},1\rangle_{\Gamma_0}=\int_\Omega q\,dx$. In particular $\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mathcal{R}_nq=0$.
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:Rd}, $\mathcal{R}_nq$ has a divergence in $L^2_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^n)$, so its normal
traces from the two sides of $\Gamma_i$ coincide, and $\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq=\widetilde q=0$
in $O_i$; since the outer normal of $O_i$ on $\Gamma_i$ is $-{\bf n}$,
$F_i(\mathcal{R}_nq)=-\int_{O_i}\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}_nq\,dx=0$. The divergence theorem in $\Omega$ gives $\sum_{i=0}^b\langle\mathcal{R}_nq\cdot{\bf n},1\rangle_{\Gamma_i}=\int_\Omega q\,dx$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:hdiv_split}
$P:=\mathcal{R}_n\operatorname{div}$ is the bounded projection of $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ onto $\mathcal{R}_nL^2(\Omega)$ along $\ker\operatorname{div}$, and
\begin{equation}\label{hdiv_split}
 H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)=\bigl(\operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_n\bigr)\oplus\mathcal{R}_nL^2(\Omega),
\end{equation}
where $\ker\operatorname{div}=\operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_n$ and
\begin{equation}\label{Div_range}
 \operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)=\{{\bf v}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega):\ \operatorname{div}{\bf v}=0,\ F_i({\bf v})=0,\ i=1,\ldots,b\}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ can be written
\begin{equation}\label{hdiv_d_representation}
 \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}+\mathcal{R}_n\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$} ,
\end{equation}
that is, with $q=\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ and $\mathbf{h}=\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:R_fluxes}), where $q$, $\mathbf{h}$ and $\operatorname{Div}A=\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mathcal{R}_nq-\mathbf{h}$
are unique and the potential $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ is unique up to
an element of the kernel of $\operatorname{Div}$. The potential can be fixed by a bounded
linear map: there is a bounded linear $\mathsf L$ from the space \eqref{Div_range}
to $H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ with $\operatorname{Div}\,\mathsf L{\bf v}={\bf v}$, and $A=\mathsf L(\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mathcal{R}_nq-\mathbf{h})$ satisfies
\[
 \|A\|_1+\|\mathbf{h}\|_0+\|q\|_0\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})} .
\]
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:Rd}, $P$ is bounded, $P^2=P$, $\operatorname{ran} P=\mathcal{R}_nL^2(\Omega)$ and $\ker P=\ker\operatorname{div}$, so $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)=\ker\operatorname{div}\oplus\mathcal{R}_nL^2(\Omega)$. It remains to show $\ker\operatorname{div}=\operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_n$ and \eqref{Div_range}, with $\|A\|_1+\|\mathbf{h}\|_0\le C\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$ for ${\bf v}\in\ker\operatorname{div}$. For
a general $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ this is applied to ${\bf v}:=\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mathcal{R}_nq$, $q=\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$, for which $\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}{\bf v}=\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ by Corollary~\ref{cor:R_fluxes}; then $\|q\|_0\le\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$ and $\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$ by \eqref{Rd_bound}. Let $O_0:=B\setminus\overline{\Omega\cup O_1\cup\cdots\cup O_b}$ for an open ball $B\supset\overline\Omega$.

\emph{The sum is direct.} For $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ let
$\widetilde A\in H^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb K_n)$ be a skew-symmetric extension:
each $a_{ij}$, $i<j$, is extended by a bounded extension
operator of the Lipschitz domain $\Omega$, and $\widetilde A_{ji}:=-\widetilde A_{ij}$.
By \eqref{divDiv_zero}, $\operatorname{Div}\widetilde A\in L^2(\mathbb{R}^n)^n$ is divergence free, so
its normal traces from the two sides of $\Gamma_i$
coincide, and $F_i(\operatorname{Div}A)=-\int_{O_i}\operatorname{div}\operatorname{Div}\widetilde A\,dx=0$, $i=1,\ldots,b$. An $\mathbf{h}\in{\mathcal{H}}_n$ with zero cavity fluxes
vanishes, since $F_i(\mathbf{h}_j)=-|S^{n-1}|\delta_{ij}$; hence $\operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)\cap{\mathcal{H}}_n=\{0\}$, and $\operatorname{Div}\,H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$
is contained in the right-hand side of \eqref{Div_range}.

\emph{Every divergence-free field is represented.} Let ${\bf v}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ with $\operatorname{div}{\bf v}=0$, and let $\mathbf{h}:=\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}{\bf v}$; then $\|\mathbf{h}\|_0\le C\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$ by the boundedness of the $F_i$. The field ${\bf w}:={\bf v}-\mathbf{h}$ is divergence free with zero flux through $\Gamma_1,\ldots,\Gamma_b$, and through $\Gamma_0$ as well, because the fluxes through all components sum to $\int_\Omega\operatorname{div}{\bf w}\,dx=0$. For $i=0,\ldots,b$ let $\phi_i\in H^1(O_i)$, unique up to a constant, solve the Neumann problem
\[
 \int_{O_i}\operatorname{grad}\phi_i\cdot\operatorname{grad}\chi\,dx=-\langle{\bf w}\cdot{\bf n},\chi\rangle_{\Gamma_i}\qquad\text{for all }\chi\in H^1(O_i),
\]
that is, $\Delta\phi_i=0$ in $O_i$, $\operatorname{grad}\phi_i\cdot{\bf n}={\bf w}\cdot{\bf n}$ on $\Gamma_i$ (the outer
normal of $O_i$ there is $-{\bf n}$) and, for $i=0$,
$\partial_\nu\phi_0=0$ on $\partial B$. The right-hand side vanishes
for $\chi=1$ because the flux of ${\bf w}$ through $\Gamma_i$ is
zero, so $\phi_i$ exists, and $\|\operatorname{grad}\phi_i\|_{0,O_i}\le C\|{\bf w}\cdot{\bf n}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma_i)}\le C\|{\bf w}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$. The field
$\widetilde{\bf w}$ equal to ${\bf w}$ in $\Omega$ and to $\operatorname{grad}\phi_i$ in
$O_i$ has the same normal trace from both sides
of every $\Gamma_i$, so $\widetilde{\bf w}\in L^2(B)^n$ is divergence free
in $B$, with $\|\widetilde{\bf w}\|_{0,B}\le C\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$. The $(n-1)$-form $\widetilde{\bf w}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$ is
closed in $B$, and $B$ is starlike with respect
to a ball, so \cite[Proposition~4.1(i)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} gives an $(n-2)$-form $\mu\in H^1(B,\Lambda^{n-2})$ with $d\mu=\widetilde{\bf w}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$
and $\|\mu\|_{1,B}\le C\|\widetilde{\bf w}\|_{0,B}$. Let $\widetilde A\in H^1(B;\mathbb K_n)$
be the skew-symmetric matrix of the $2$-form with $\star\widetilde A=\mu$.
By Remark~\ref{rem:forms}, $(\operatorname{Div}\widetilde A)\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}=d(\star\widetilde A)=\widetilde{\bf w}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$, so $\operatorname{Div}\widetilde A=\widetilde{\bf w}$, and
$\|\widetilde A\|_{1,B}\le C\|\widetilde{\bf w}\|_{0,B}$. Then $A:=\widetilde A|_\Omega$ satisfies $\operatorname{Div}A={\bf v}-\mathbf{h}$ and $\|A\|_1\le C\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$. If
$F_i({\bf v})=0$ for all $i$, then $\mathbf{h}=0$ and ${\bf v}=\operatorname{Div}A$, which gives the
other inclusion in \eqref{Div_range}. For $n=2$ and $n=3$ this
is the classical argument for the stream function
and the vector potential \cite[Lemmas~2.1.2 and~2.1.9]{BGHRR:25}. Every step is linear in ${\bf v}$:
the Neumann problems with $\phi_i$ normalized by zero mean, the extension
$\widetilde{\bf w}$, and the integral operator of \cite[Proposition~4.1(i)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010}; restricted to the space
\eqref{Div_range}, where $\mathbf{h}=0$, this is the map $\mathsf L$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- cor-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:representation_norm}
For $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+\mathcal{R}_nq+\mathbf{h}$ as in Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_split}, with any potential $A$,
\[
 c\,\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\operatorname{dist}_{H^1}(A,\ker\operatorname{Div})+\|\mathbf{h}\|_0+\|q\|_0\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})} ,
\]
and the same holds for $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m$ with $A-A_m$, $\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}_m$, $q-q_m$ in place of $A$, $\mathbf{h}$, $q$.
<!-- end cor-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
For $K\in\ker\operatorname{Div}$, $\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\|\operatorname{Div}(A-K)\|_0+\|\mathbf{h}\|_0+\|\mathcal{R}_nq\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\sqrt{n-1}\,|A-K|_1+\|\mathbf{h}\|_0+C\|q\|_0$ by \eqref{divDiv_zero} and \eqref{Rd_bound}. Conversely, $\|q\|_0\le\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$, $\|\mathbf{h}\|_0\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$ by the boundedness of the $F_i$, and $A-\mathsf L\,\operatorname{Div}A\in\ker\operatorname{Div}$, so $\operatorname{dist}_{H^1}(A,\ker\operatorname{Div})\le\|\mathsf L(\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mathcal{R}_nq-\mathbf{h})\|_1\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$. The statement for $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m$ follows by linearity.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:regular_potential}
Let $r\ge0$, ${\bf u}\in H^r(\Omega)^n$ with $\operatorname{div}{\bf u}=0$ and $F_i({\bf u})=0$, $i=1,\ldots,b$. Then ${\bf u}=\operatorname{Div}A$ with $A\in H^{r+1}(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $\|A\|_{r+1}\le C_r\|{\bf u}\|_r$.
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
By \eqref{Div_range} and Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_split}, ${\bf u}=\operatorname{Div}A_0$ with $A_0\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb
K_n)$ and $\|A_0\|_1\le C\|{\bf u}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}=C\|{\bf u}\|_0\le C\|{\bf u}\|_r$. By Remark~\ref{rem:forms}, ${\bf u}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}=d\mu_0$ with $\mu_0:=\star A_0\in
H^1(\Omega,\Lambda^{n-2})$. By \cite[Corollary~4.7(a)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} with $s=r$ and $t=1$ there is
$\mu\in H^{r+1}(\Omega,\Lambda^{n-2})$ with $d\mu={\bf u}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$ and $\|\mu\|_{r+1}\le C_r(\|{\bf u}\|_r+\|\mu_0\|_1)\le C_r\|{\bf u}\|_r$.
The skew-symmetric matrix $A$ of the $2$-form with $\star
A=\mu$ satisfies $\operatorname{Div}A={\bf u}$ by Remark~\ref{rem:forms}, and $\|A\|_{r+1}\le C_r\|{\bf u}\|_r$. The
splitting removes the topological obstruction, and \cite[Corollary~4.7(a)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} raises the regularity.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:hdivd}
Every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{Div}A_m+\mathcal{R}_nq_m+\mathbf{h}\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}$ lies in $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, and $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=q_m$. Let $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, $q=\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$, $\mathbf{h}=\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$, and let $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ be any potential with $\operatorname{Div}A=\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mathcal{R}_nq-\mathbf{h}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{hdiv_d_approx}
 \inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0\le\inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}
 \le\sqrt{n-1}\inf_{A_m\in{\mathcal A}_m}|A-A_m|_1+\Bigl(1+\frac{4C_{x_0}^2}{n^2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Conformity and the divergence identity follow from \eqref{divDiv_zero}, Lemma~\ref{lem:Rd} and $\operatorname{div}\mathbf{h}=0$. The lower bound holds because $\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\ge\|\operatorname{div}(\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m)\|_0=\|q-q_m\|_0$ for every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}$. For the upper bound let $A_m\in{\mathcal A}_m$ and $q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m$ be arbitrary and $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m:=\operatorname{Div}A_m+\mathcal{R}_nq_m+\mathbf{h}$, with the same $\mathbf{h}$ as $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$. Then $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{Div}(A-A_m)+\mathcal{R}_n(q-q_m)$. The first term is divergence free, so its $H(\operatorname{div})$ norm is $\|\operatorname{Div}(A-A_m)\|_0\le\sqrt{n-1}|A-A_m|_1$ by \eqref{divDiv_zero}; the second is bounded by $(1+4C_{x_0}^2/n^2)^{1/2}\|q-q_m\|_0$ by \eqref{Rd_bound}. The triangle inequality and independent minimization over $A_m$ and $q_m$ give the upper bound.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- cor-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:hdiv_density}
If $\bigcup_m{\mathcal A}_m$ is dense in $H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $\bigcup_m{\mathcal{Q}}_m$ is dense in $L^2(\Omega)$, then $\bigcup_m\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}$ is dense in $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, and $\inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\to0$ as $m\to\infty$ for every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$.
<!-- end cor-4 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_split} every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ has a representation with $A$, $\mathbf{h}$, $q$; the
two infima in \eqref{hdiv_d_approx} are nonincreasing in $m$ because the families are nested,
and they tend to zero by the density assumptions.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- rem-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:RT0}
The splitting mirrors the local polynomial decomposition of the Raviart--Thomas spaces; on a simplex the topological component ${\mathcal{H}}_n$ is absent. Let $T\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ be a simplex and $x_T\in T$ a center chosen locally in $T$, independently of the global center $x_0$
of $\mathcal{R}_n$; let $\mathcal{R}_{n,T}$ denote \eqref{Rd} on $T$ with center $x_T$. Let $\mathbb P_k$ be the polynomials of total degree at most $k$, $\widetilde{\mathbb P}_k$ those homogeneous of degree $k$ in $x-x_T$, and $\mathbb K_n(\mathbb P_{k+1})$ the skew-symmetric matrices with entries in $\mathbb P_{k+1}$. Then $RT_k(T)=\mathbb P_k^n\oplus(x-x_T)\widetilde{\mathbb P}_k$, and
\[
 RT_k(T)=\operatorname{Div}\,\mathbb K_n(\mathbb P_{k+1})\oplus(x-x_T)\mathbb P_k\qquad\text{for every }k\ge0
\]
(the Koszul decomposition, \cite{AFW:2006}): an element $\mathbf p+(x-x_T)\widetilde p$, $\widetilde p\in\widetilde{\mathbb P}_k$, has divergence $\operatorname{div}\mathbf p+(n+k)\widetilde p$, so
its divergence-free elements lie in $\mathbb P_k^n$, and the divergence-free fields in $\mathbb P_k^n$ are $\operatorname{Div}\,\mathbb K_n(\mathbb P_{k+1})$
by the exactness of the polynomial de Rham complex \cite{AFW:2006}; the second part carries the divergence. For polynomials $\mathcal{R}_{n,T}$ produces exactly the second part: $T$
is convex and contains $x_T$, so the segments from $x_T$ to the points of $T$ stay
in $T$, and for $q_j$ homogeneous of degree $j$ in $x-x_T$, $\mathcal{R}_{n,T}q_j(x)=(x-x_T)\,q_j(x)\int_0^1t^{n-1+j}\,dt=(x-x_T)\,q_j(x)/(n+j)$. Hence $\operatorname{Div}A+\mathcal{R}_{n,T}q$ with $A\in\mathbb K_n(\mathbb P_{k+1})$ and $q\in\mathbb P_k$ is exactly $RT_k(T)$, and the neural
classes replace the polynomials $A$ and $q$ by networks. On a triangulation the corresponding global statement is made in two dimensions only.
<!-- end rem-3 -->
<!-- cor-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:hdiv23}
Let $n\in\{2,3\}$. Every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ can be written
\begin{equation}\label{hdiv_23_representation}
 \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi+\mathcal{R}_2q+\mathbf{h}\quad(n=2),\qquad \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{curl}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}+\mathcal{R}_3q+\mathbf{h}\quad(n=3),\qquad q=\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$},\quad \mathbf{h}=\Pi_{{\mathcal{H}}_n}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$},
\end{equation}
where $q$ and $\mathbf{h}$ are unique ($\mathbf{h}$ is determined by the hole or cavity fluxes of $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$, Corollary~\ref{cor:R_fluxes}), $\psi\in H^1(\Omega)$ is unique
up to a constant and $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$ up to an element of the kernel of $\operatorname{curl}$, and they can be chosen with $\|\psi\|_1$, respectively $\|\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}\|_1$,
plus $\|\mathbf{h}\|_0+\|q\|_0$ bounded by $C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$. Every element of $\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}$ lies in $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, with divergence $q_m$, and for every such $\psi$ or $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}$
\begin{equation}\label{derham_density_bound}
\begin{aligned}
 &\inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0\le\inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm dR}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\\
 &\qquad\le\begin{cases}
 \inf_{\psi_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\psi-\psi_m|_1+\sqrt{1+C_{x_0}^2}\,\inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0, & n=2,\\[2pt]
 \sqrt2\inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_m\in{\mathcal{N}}_m}|\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_m|_1+\bigl(1+\tfrac49C_{x_0}^2\bigr)^{1/2}\inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0, & n=3;
 \end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
for $n=2$ the first term on the right does not depend on the choice of $\psi$.
<!-- end cor-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_split} and Theorem~\ref{thm:hdivd} with $A=\psi\mathcal E_{12}$ ($n=2$) and $A_{ij}=\sum_k\varepsilon_{ijk}\Psi_k$ ($n=3$):
$\operatorname{Div}A$ is $\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi$, respectively $\operatorname{curl}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}$, so the kernel of $\operatorname{Div}$ corresponds to that of the curl (the constants for
$n=2$, $\Omega$ being connected), $|A|_1=|\psi|_1$, respectively $|\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}|_1$, and $\sqrt{n-1}$ and $(1+4C_{x_0}^2/n^2)^{1/2}$ give the constants; the
seminorm $|\cdot|_1$ is invariant under constants.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- rem-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:RT0_2d}
On a conforming triangulation $\mathcal T_h$ of a simply connected $\Omega$, $RT_0(\mathcal T_h)\cap\ker\operatorname{div}=\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}} S_1(\mathcal T_h)$,
$S_1(\mathcal T_h)$ the continuous piecewise linear functions, and every function in $S_1(\mathcal T_h)$ is a ReLU network
\cite{HLXZ:20}; hence $\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}} S_1(\mathcal T_h)\subset\Sigma_{m,2}^{\rm dR}$ for a network size depending only on $\mathcal T_h$, whereas a
componentwise network with continuous activations is a continuous vector field, so for no network size does it contain a field of
$\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}} S_1(\mathcal T_h)$ with a tangential jump; it can only approximate such a field.  For a general $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_h\in RT_0(\mathcal T_h)$, $\mathcal{R}_2q_h$ with $q_h=\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_h$ piecewise constant need not be
piecewise polynomial, so exact finite realization is not asserted; Corollary~\ref{cor:hdiv23} gives
$\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_h=\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi_h+\mathcal{R}_2q_h$ with $\psi_h\in H^1(\Omega)$, and by \eqref{derham_density_bound} fitting $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_h$ is a scalar
approximation problem for $\psi_h$ and $q_h$.  In three dimensions, on a topologically trivial domain, the divergence-free fields of
$RT_0(\mathcal T_h)$ are the curls of lowest-order N\'ed\'elec fields, which in general are not continuous; on a general domain the
corresponding discrete harmonic fields must be added.
<!-- end rem-4 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:hdiv_regular}
Every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ can be written $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+{\bf z}$ with $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^n$ depending linearly
on $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ and $\|A\|_1+\|{\bf z}\|_1\le C\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}$; for $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H_0(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ the same holds with $A\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and
${\bf z}\in H^1_0(\Omega)^n$.  Then $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{div}{\bf z}$, and no cohomology part appears: the harmonic fields are absorbed into ${\bf z}$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By Remark~\ref{rem:forms}, $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ corresponds to the $(n-1)$-form $\omega=\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$, with $\mathrm{d}\omega=(\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})\,\mathrm{vol}$, and an
$(n-2)$-form $\mu$ to the skew-symmetric matrix $A$ with $\star A=\mu$, with $\mathrm{d}\mu=(\operatorname{Div}A)\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}$.  By
\cite[Theorem~4.6]{CostabelMcIntosh2010} there are operators $R_\ell$ (regularized Poincar\'e operators, not to be confused with $\mathcal{R}_n$),
bounded from $H^s\Lambda^\ell(\Omega)$ to $H^{s+1}\Lambda^{\ell-1}(\Omega)$ for every $s$, and $K_\ell$, bounded from $H^s\Lambda^\ell(\Omega)$ to
$C^\infty\Lambda^\ell(\overline\Omega)$, with $\mathrm{d} R_\ell u+R_{\ell+1}\mathrm{d} u=u-K_\ell u$.  For $u=\omega$ take $\mu=R_{n-1}\omega$, which is in $H^1$, and
${\bf z}\lrcorner\,\mathrm{vol}=R_n\mathrm{d}\omega+K_{n-1}\omega$, which is in $H^1$ since $\mathrm{d}\omega\in L^2$.  For $H_0(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ the Bogovski\u{\i}-type
operators $T_\ell$, $L_\ell$ of the same theorem, applied to the extension of $\omega$ by zero, give the same identity with supports in
$\overline\Omega$.  Taking the divergence of $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+{\bf z}$ gives $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{div}{\bf z}$ by \eqref{divDiv_zero}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:hdivd_reg}
Every $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{Div}A_m+{\bf z}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm reg}$ lies in $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, and $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{div}{\bf z}_m$.  For $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ and any
$A$, ${\bf z}$ as in Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_regular},
\begin{equation}\label{hdiv_reg_approx}
 \inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\in\Sigma_{m,n}^{\rm reg}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\sqrt{n-1}\inf_{A_m\in{\mathcal A}_m}|A-A_m|_1+\sqrt n\inf_{{\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m}\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_1 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
$\operatorname{div}\operatorname{Div}A_m=0$ by \eqref{divDiv_zero}, and ${\bf z}_m\in H^1(\Omega)^n\subset H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$.  For $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{Div}A_m+{\bf z}_m$,
$\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m=\operatorname{Div}(A-A_m)+({\bf z}-{\bf z}_m)$; the first term is divergence free, with $\|\operatorname{Div}(A-A_m)\|_0\le\sqrt{n-1}|A-A_m|_1$
by \eqref{divDiv_zero}, and for ${\bf w}\in H^1(\Omega)^n$, $\|\operatorname{div}{\bf w}\|_0\le\sqrt n|{\bf w}|_1$, so $\|{\bf w}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\sqrt n\|{\bf w}\|_1$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:jump}
Let $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+{\bf w}$ as in \eqref{hdiv_common_form} with $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$, let $\Gamma\subset\Omega$ be a smooth hypersurface with unit normal ${\bf n}$,
let $A$ be piecewise $H^2$ on the two sides of $\Gamma$ in a neighborhood of $\Gamma$, and let ${\bf w}$ have no jump across $\Gamma$, its one-sided traces coinciding.  (For ${\bf w}=\mathcal{R}_nq+\mathbf{h}$ this holds by Lemma~\ref{lem:Rcont} if $q$ is
bounded and piecewise continuous and the condition on the segments of that lemma is satisfied, $\mathbf{h}$ being smooth; for ${\bf w}={\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^n$
the trace on $\Gamma$ is single valued.)  Then, with one-sided traces,
\[
 [\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\cdot{\bf n}]\!]=0,\qquad [\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]\,{\bf n} .
\]
Thus the normal continuity required by $H(\operatorname{div})$ is automatic, and the tangential jump of $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ is carried entirely
by the kink of $A$. Conversely, pointwise on $\Gamma$, every admissible jump $\mathbf j$ ($\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n}=0$) is produced by the
kink $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]=\mathbf j{\bf n}^T-{\bf n}\mathbf j^T$; two kinks that produce the same jump differ by a skew-symmetric $K$ with $K{\bf n}=0$, which vanishes for $n=2$ and ranges over a space
of dimension $(n-1)(n-2)/2$ for $n\ge3$. The statement holds for every potential of $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$ that is
piecewise $H^2$ near $\Gamma$; since $[\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}]\!]$ does not depend on the potential, the kinks of two such potentials differ by such a $K$.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Since ${\bf w}$ has no jump across $\Gamma$, $[\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}]\!]=[\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:skew}(b)
gives $[\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]{\bf n}$ and ${\bf n}\cdot[\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=0$. The converse and the nonuniqueness are Lemma~\ref{lem:skew}(c).
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- cor-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:cpwl}
Let the entries $a_{ij}$, $i<j$, of $A$ be continuous and piecewise affine on a finite partition of $\Omega$ into polyhedral cells; for finite ReLU networks this is the common refinement of the activation
partitions of the entries. Then $\operatorname{Div}A$ is piecewise constant, belongs to $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ with $\operatorname{div}\operatorname{Div}A=0$, and across the relative interior of every interior codimension-one face, with unit normal ${\bf n}$,
\[
 {\bf n}\cdot[\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=0,\qquad [\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]\,{\bf n} .
\]
<!-- end cor-6 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
$A\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ (continuous and piecewise affine on a finite polyhedral partition), hence
$A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:skew}(a) applies; on the relative interior
of a face, $A$ is affine on each side, and Lemma~\ref{lem:skew}(b) applies.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:mult_kink}
Let $\rho\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$, $N\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $B:=\rho N$. Then $B\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$, and
$\operatorname{Div}(A_0+B)+{\bf w}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ for every $A_0\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and every remaining part ${\bf w}$ of either
class. Let further $x^*\in\Omega$, let $\Gamma$ be a hypersurface of class $C^1$
near $x^*$ with $x^*\in\Gamma$ and unit normal ${\bf n}$,
let $\rho$ vanish on $\Gamma$ and be continuously differentiable on each side
of $\Gamma$ near $x^*$, up to $\Gamma$, with one-sided gradients $\operatorname{grad}\rho^\pm$, and let $N$ be
Lipschitz continuous near $x^*$. Then, in the sense of one-sided essential limits at $x^*$,
\[
 [\![ \operatorname{grad} B]\!]=N(x^*)\otimes[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!],\qquad
 [\![ \operatorname{Div}B]\!]=N(x^*)[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\rho]\!]\,N(x^*){\bf n},\qquad {\bf n}\cdot[\![ \operatorname{Div}B]\!]=0,
\]
with $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\rho]\!]{\bf n}$ evaluated at $x^*$, and the same identities
hold for $A_0+B$ if $A_0$ is continuously differentiable near $x^*$.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Since $\rho\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$ and $N\in H^1$, $B\in H^1$ with $\operatorname{grad}(\rho N_{ij})=N_{ij}\operatorname{grad}\rho+\rho\operatorname{grad} N_{ij}$ almost everywhere, and conformity follows from \eqref{divDiv_zero} and Lemma~\ref{lem:Rd}. Near $x^*$,
$N$ is continuous and $|\operatorname{grad} N|\le L$ almost everywhere ($L$ a Lipschitz constant), and $\rho$ is continuous with $\rho(x^*)=0$, so
$\operatorname{ess\,sup}|\rho\operatorname{grad} N|\le L\sup|\rho|\to0$ on shrinking neighborhoods of $x^*$; the first term has the one-sided limits $N_{ij}(x^*)\operatorname{grad}\rho^\pm(x^*)$. Since $\rho=0$ on $\Gamma$, its tangential derivatives vanish from both
sides, so $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\rho]\!]{\bf n}$, and ${\bf n}\cdot N{\bf n}=0$ because $N$ is skew. Where $A_0$ is continuously differentiable, its gradient adds no jump.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:interface_module}
Let $A=A_0+|d_\Gamma|N$ with $A_0,N\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} applies with $\rho=|d_\Gamma|$ and $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=2\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$: $A\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb
K_n)$, $\operatorname{Div}A+{\bf w}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ for every remaining part ${\bf w}$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink}, and at every
$x^*\in\Gamma$ near which $N$ is Lipschitz continuous and $A_0$ is continuously differentiable,
\[
 [\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|\,N,\qquad [\![ \operatorname{Div}A]\!]=2N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|\,N{\bf n} .
\]
Every Lipschitz tangential jump field is attained: let $d_\Gamma$ be in $C^{1,1}$ near $\Gamma$, let $\mathbf j:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^n$ be Lipschitz with $\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n}=0$, let $U$ be a neighborhood of $\Gamma$ on which
$|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|$ is bounded away from zero, $\widetilde{\mathbf j}$ a Lipschitz extension of $\mathbf j$ to $U$ (componentwise), $\widehat{\bf n}:=\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma/|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|$ on $U$, and $\chi$ a Lipschitz cutoff with $\chi=1$ on $\Gamma$ and support in $U$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{module_jump_field}
 N:=\frac{\chi}{2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf j}\,\widehat{\bf n}^T-\widehat{\bf n}\,\widetilde{\mathbf j}^T\bigr)
\end{equation}
(extended by $0$ outside $U$) is skew-symmetric and Lipschitz, and $[\![ \operatorname{Div}(|d_\Gamma|N)]\!]=\mathbf j$ at every point of $\Gamma$. The geometry is fixed and the jump amplitude is trainable.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
On the two sides of $\Gamma$, $|d_\Gamma|=\pm d_\Gamma$ is continuously differentiable up to $\Gamma$ with gradients $\pm\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$, so $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=2\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$ and $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\rho]\!]=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} gives the identities. For \eqref{module_jump_field}: $\widetilde{\mathbf j}$, $\widehat{\bf n}$, $1/|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|$ and $\chi$ are bounded and
Lipschitz on $U$, so $N$ is Lipschitz and the identities hold at every point of $\Gamma$; there $\chi=1$ and $\widehat{\bf n}={\bf n}$, so $2N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma=(\mathbf j{\bf n}^T-{\bf n}\mathbf j^T){\bf n}=\mathbf j-{\bf n}(\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n})=\mathbf j$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- cor-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:jump_amplitude}
Let $U$ be a bounded Lipschitz domain with $\Gamma\subset\partial U$ (a
one-sided neighborhood of $\Gamma$), let $|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|\le G$ on $\Gamma$, and for $M\in H^1(U;\mathbb K_n)$ let
$\mathcal J_\Gamma(M):=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|\,M{\bf n}\in L^2(\Gamma)^n$, with the trace of $M$. Then
\[
 \|\mathcal J_\Gamma(M)-\mathcal J_\Gamma(M')\|_{L^2(\Gamma)}\le 2\sqrt2\,G\,C_{\rm tr}\,\|M-M'\|_{H^1(U)}
\]
for $M,M'\in H^1(U;\mathbb K_n)$, with $C_{\rm tr}$ the constant of the trace inequality $\|w\|_{L^2(\Gamma)}\le C_{\rm tr}\|w\|_{H^1(U)}$. In particular, if $N$ is the amplitude \eqref{module_jump_field} of a jump $\mathbf j$ and $N_m$ are Lipschitz networks, then $[\![ \operatorname{Div}(|d_\Gamma|N_m)]\!]=\mathcal J_\Gamma(N_m)$ and $\|[\![ \operatorname{Div}(|d_\Gamma|N_m)]\!]-\mathbf j\|_{L^2(\Gamma)}\le2\sqrt2\,G\,C_{\rm tr}\|N-N_m\|_{H^1(U)}$.
<!-- end cor-7 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
$\mathcal J_\Gamma$ is linear and $|M{\bf n}|\le(\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}^2)^{1/2}=\sqrt2\,(\sum_{i<j}M_{ij}^2)^{1/2}$ pointwise; apply the trace inequality to each
entry. The identity for $N_m$ is Proposition~\ref{prop:interface_module} with $A_0=0$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- rem-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:kellogg_ansatz}
Several fixed interfaces are treated by one module each, and where two of them
cross a product term is added. For the interfaces $\{x_1=0\}$ and
$\{x_2=0\}$ of the Kellogg checkerboard problem \cite{Kellogg:74,Zhang:kellogg} the two-dimensional potential is
\begin{equation}\label{kellogg_psi}
 \psi_\theta=N_0+|x_1|N_1+|x_2|N_2+|x_1||x_2|N_3 ,
\end{equation}
and the same form $v_\theta=V_0+|x_1|V_1+|x_2|V_2+|x_1||x_2|V_3$ is used for the scalar solution, which is continuous with coefficient-induced derivative jumps. On $\{x_1=0\}$, $[\![ \partial_{x_1}\psi_\theta]\!]=2N_1(0,x_2)+2|x_2|N_3(0,x_2)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} with $\rho=|x_1|$ and the amplitude $N_1+|x_2|N_3$), and $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi_\theta\cdot{\bf t}]\!]$ is minus this (Corollary~\ref{cor:jump2d}); similarly on $\{x_2=0\}$. The two break lines are present for every parameter value. The singular behavior at the cross point, $u\sim r^\alpha\varphi(\vartheta)$ with $0<\alpha<1$, is left to the trainable networks.
<!-- end rem-5 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:regularity_recovery}
Let $\Gamma$ divide $\Omega$ into Lipschitz subdomains $\Omega_\pm$, let $d_\Gamma\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$ with $d_\Gamma|_{U_\Gamma}\in C^{1,1}(U_\Gamma)$ on a neighborhood $U_\Gamma$ of
$\Gamma$, $\Gamma\subset\{d_\Gamma=0\}$, $\pm d_\Gamma>0$ on $\Omega_\pm\cap U_\Gamma$, and $|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|$ bounded away from zero on $\Gamma$, and let ${\bf v}\in
H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ satisfy $\operatorname{div}{\bf v}=0$, $F_i({\bf v})=0$, $i=1,\ldots,b$, and ${\bf v}|_{\Omega_\pm}\in H^1(\Omega_\pm)^n$, with jump $\mathbf
j:=[\![ {\bf v}]\!]$ on $\Gamma$. If $N\in H^2(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ is supported in $U_\Gamma$ and $2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N{\bf n}=\mathbf j$ on $\Gamma$, then
\[
 {\bf v}=\operatorname{Div}\bigl(A_0+|d_\Gamma|N\bigr),\qquad A_0\in H^2(\Omega;\mathbb K_n),\qquad \|A_0\|_2\le C\|{\bf v}-\operatorname{Div}(|d_\Gamma|N)\|_1 .
\]
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Let ${\bf w}:=\operatorname{Div}(|d_\Gamma|N)$. Since $|d_\Gamma|N\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$, ${\bf w}$ lies in the space \eqref{Div_range}: $\operatorname{div}{\bf w}=0$ and $F_i({\bf w})=0$.
On $\Omega_\pm$, $|d_\Gamma|N=\pm d_\Gamma N\in H^2$, so ${\bf w}$ is in $H^1$ on both sides, and ${\bf w}=N\operatorname{grad}|d_\Gamma|+|d_\Gamma|\,\operatorname{Div}N$ has the
traces $\pm N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$ on $\Gamma$, since $|d_\Gamma|=0$ there; hence $[\![ {\bf w}]\!]=2N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N{\bf n}=\mathbf j$. The field ${\bf v}_0:={\bf v}-{\bf w}$ is divergence
free, has zero cavity fluxes, is in $H^1$ on both sides and has no jump across
$\Gamma$, so ${\bf v}_0\in H^1(\Omega)^n$ (a function that is in $H^1$ on both sides of a Lipschitz interface and has equal
traces there is in $H^1$). Corollary~\ref{cor:regular_potential} with $r=1$ gives ${\bf v}_0=\operatorname{Div}A_0$ with $A_0\in H^2(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $\|A_0\|_2\le C_1\|{\bf v}_0\|_1$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- cor-8: body_md -->
\label{cor:enriched_hdiv}
Let $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}(A_0+|d_\Gamma|N)+{\bf w}$ with $A_0,N\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$, $d_\Gamma\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$ and a remaining part ${\bf w}$ of either
class, and let the class consist of $\operatorname{Div}(A_{0,m}+|d_\Gamma|N_m)+{\bf w}_m$ with $A_{0,m}\in{\mathcal A}_m$,
$N_m\in{\mathcal A}_m'$, $\{{\mathcal A}_m'\}$ a second family as in Section~\ref{subsec:hdiv_classes}, and ${\bf w}_m=\mathcal{R}_nq_m+\mathbf{h}$ with $q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m$, respectively ${\bf w}_m={\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m$. Then
\[
 \inf_{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}\le\sqrt{n-1}\Bigl(\inf_{A_{0,m}\in{\mathcal A}_m}|A_0-A_{0,m}|_1+C_d\inf_{N_m\in{\mathcal A}_m'}\|N-N_m\|_1\Bigr)+E_{{\bf w}},
\]
with $C_d:=\|d_\Gamma\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}+\|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}$ and $E_{{\bf w}}=(1+4C_{x_0}^2/n^2)^{1/2}\inf_{q_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|q-q_m\|_0$ in the first class, $E_{{\bf w}}=\sqrt n\inf_{{\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m}\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_1$ in the second.
<!-- end cor-8 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The upper bounds in the proofs of Theorems~\ref{thm:hdivd}
and~\ref{thm:hdivd_reg} hold for every $A_m$, in particular for
$A_m=A_{0,m}+|d_\Gamma|N_m$. Since $\operatorname{grad}(|d_\Gamma|M)=\operatorname{sign}(d_\Gamma)\,M\otimes\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma+|d_\Gamma|\operatorname{grad} M$ almost everywhere, $\bigl||d_\Gamma|M\bigr|_1\le
C_d\|M\|_1$, which applied to $M=N-N_m$ gives the estimate.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:learned_interface_hdiv}
Let $g_\eta\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$, $A_{0,\theta},N_\theta\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $B_{\theta,\eta}:=\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)N_\theta$.
Then $A_{\theta,\eta}\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb K_n)$ and $\operatorname{Div}A_{\theta,\eta}+{\bf w}\in H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$ for
every $(\theta,\eta)$ and every remaining part ${\bf w}$ as
in Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink}, also where $\Gamma_\eta:=\{g_\eta=0\}$ has singular points,
branches, or points with $\operatorname{grad} g_\eta=0$; moving the learned
interface never destroys $H(\operatorname{div})$ conformity. Let $x^*\in\Gamma_\eta$ be
a regular point: $g_\eta$ is continuously differentiable near
$x^*$ (for a ReLU network $g_\eta$: $x^*$ away from
its own activation interfaces) with $\operatorname{grad} g_\eta(x^*)\ne0$, and
$N_\theta$ is Lipschitz continuous near $x^*$. With the
$+$ side $\{g_\eta>0\}$ and ${\bf n}_\eta=\operatorname{grad} g_\eta/|\operatorname{grad} g_\eta|$, in
the sense of one-sided essential limits at $x^*$,
\begin{equation}\label{learned_interface_Div_jump}
 [\![ \operatorname{grad} B_{\theta,\eta}]\!]=N_\theta\otimes\operatorname{grad} g_\eta,\qquad
 [\![ \operatorname{Div}B_{\theta,\eta}]\!]=N_\theta\operatorname{grad} g_\eta=|\operatorname{grad} g_\eta|\,N_\theta{\bf n}_\eta,\qquad
 {\bf n}_\eta\cdot[\![ \operatorname{Div}B_{\theta,\eta}]\!]=0,
\end{equation}
and the same identities hold for $A_{\theta,\eta}$ if $A_{0,\theta}$ is continuously differentiable near $x^*$. At each regular point the jump map $N\mapsto N\operatorname{grad} g_\eta(x^*)$ is onto ${\bf n}_\eta^\perp$: for
$\mathbf j\cdot{\bf n}_\eta=0$, $N=|\operatorname{grad} g_\eta(x^*)|^{-1}(\mathbf j{\bf n}_\eta^T-{\bf n}_\eta\mathbf j^T)$ gives $N\operatorname{grad} g_\eta(x^*)=\mathbf j$. This is a pointwise statement; it does not assert that a fixed finite network realizes a prescribed jump field exactly along $\Gamma_\eta$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
$\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$, so the first part of Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} gives conformity for every $(\theta,\eta)$; it uses neither the zero set nor its regularity. Near a regular point, $\Gamma_\eta$ is a $C^1$ hypersurface, $\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)$ equals $g_\eta$ on $\{g_\eta>0\}$ and $0$ on $\{g_\eta<0\}$, both continuously differentiable up to $\Gamma_\eta$, so $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=\operatorname{grad} g_\eta(x^*)$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} gives the identities.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- cor-9: body_md -->
\label{cor:jump2d}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $\psi$ is continuous and piecewise affine on a finite polygonal partition (for instance a finite ReLU
network), then $\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi$ is piecewise constant, belongs to $H(\operatorname{div};\Omega)$, and across
the relative interior of every interior edge $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi\cdot{\bf n}]\!]=0$ and $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi\cdot{\bf t}]\!]=-[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\psi]\!]$.
\item[(b)] Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:jump}, with $A=\psi\mathcal E_{12}$: $[\![ \partial_{{\bf t}}\psi]\!]=0$,
$[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\psi]\!]=-[\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\cdot{\bf t}]\!]$ and $[\![ \mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\cdot{\bf n}]\!]=0$; the kink of $\psi$ is determined by the tangential jump of $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}$.
\item[(c)] At points of $\Gamma$ near which $N_1$ is Lipschitz continuous and $N_0$ is continuously differentiable,
$\psi=N_0+|d_\Gamma|N_1$ has $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\psi]\!]=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N_1$ and $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi\cdot{\bf t}]\!]=-2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N_1$ ($2N_1$ and $-2N_1$ for a signed distance). For
\begin{equation}\label{learned_interface_psi_2d}
 \psi_{\theta,\eta}=N_{0,\theta}+\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)\,N_{1,\theta},
\end{equation}
at a regular point of $\Gamma_\eta$ near which
$N_{1,\theta}$ is Lipschitz continuous and $N_{0,\theta}$ is continuously differentiable,
with ${\bf t}_\eta=(-n_{\eta,2},n_{\eta,1})^T$, $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\psi_{\theta,\eta}]\!]=N_{1,\theta}\operatorname{grad} g_\eta$, $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi_{\theta,\eta}\cdot{\bf n}_\eta]\!]=0$ and $[\![ \operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi_{\theta,\eta}\cdot{\bf t}_\eta]\!]=-N_{1,\theta}|\operatorname{grad} g_\eta|$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end cor-9 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Corollary~\ref{lem:cpwl}, Proposition~\ref{prop:jump} and Propositions~\ref{prop:interface_module} and~\ref{prop:learned_interface_hdiv} with $A=\psi\mathcal E_{12}$, $N=N_1\mathcal E_{12}$: $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\psi]\!]\mathcal E_{12}$ and $\mathcal E_{12}{\bf n}=-{\bf t}$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- cor-10: body_md -->
\label{cor:jump3d}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$ is piecewise $H^2$ on the two sides of a smooth interface with unit normal ${\bf n}$ (or continuous and
piecewise affine, across the relative interior of every interior face), then $[\![ \operatorname{curl}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}]\!]={\bf n}\times[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}]\!]$.
\item[(b)] For $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_0+|d_\Gamma|\mathbf N$ with $d_\Gamma$ as in Section~\ref{subsec:interface}, at points of $\Gamma$ near which $\mathbf N$ is Lipschitz
continuous and $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_0$ is continuously differentiable, $[\![ \operatorname{curl}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}]\!]=2\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma\times\mathbf N$.
\item[(c)] For $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_{\theta,\eta}=\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_{0,\theta}+\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)\mathbf N_\theta$, at regular points of $\Gamma_\eta$ near which $\mathbf N_\theta$ is Lipschitz
continuous and $\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_{0,\theta}$ is continuously differentiable, $[\![ \operatorname{curl}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}_{\theta,\eta}]\!]=\operatorname{grad} g_\eta\times\mathbf N_\theta$.
\end{enumerate}
All these jumps are tangential.
<!-- end cor-10 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
With $A_{ij}=\sum_k\varepsilon_{ijk}\Psi_k$ and $N_{ij}=\sum_k\varepsilon_{ijk}N_k$, $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}A]\!]{\bf n}={\bf n}\times[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\mbox{\boldmath$\Psi$}]\!]$ and $N{\bf v}={\bf v}\times\mathbf N$; (a) is Lemma~\ref{lem:skew}(b) and Corollary~\ref{lem:cpwl}, (b) and (c) are Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink} with $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=2\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$ and $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=\operatorname{grad} g_\eta$ (Propositions~\ref{prop:interface_module} and~\ref{prop:learned_interface_hdiv}).
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:div_kink}
Let $\rho\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$, ${\bf z}_0,\mathbf{Z}_1\in H^1(\Omega)^n$ and ${\bf z}:={\bf z}_0+\rho\mathbf{Z}_1$. Then ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^n$. Let
further $x^*\in\Omega$, $\Gamma$, ${\bf n}$ and $\rho$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink}, let $\mathbf{Z}_1$
be Lipschitz continuous and ${\bf z}_0$ continuously differentiable near $x^*$. Then,
in the sense of one-sided essential limits at $x^*$, $[\![ {\bf z}]\!]=0$ and
\[
 [\![ \operatorname{div}{\bf z}]\!]=\mathbf{Z}_1(x^*)\cdot[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\rho]\!]\,\mathbf{Z}_1(x^*)\cdot{\bf n} .
\]
For $\rho=|d_\Gamma|$ this is $2\mathbf{Z}_1\cdot\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$, and for $\rho=\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)$ it is $\mathbf{Z}_1\cdot\operatorname{grad} g_\eta$. If $d_\Gamma\in C^{1,1}$ near $\Gamma$, every Lipschitz jump $j$ on $\Gamma$ is attained with the Lipschitz amplitude $\mathbf{Z}_1=\chi\,\tilde j\,\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma/(2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|^2)$, $\tilde j$ a Lipschitz extension of $j$ and $\chi$ a cutoff as in Proposition~\ref{prop:interface_module}.
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
As in Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink}, applied to the components of ${\bf z}$: $\operatorname{grad}(\rho\mathbf{Z}_1)=\mathbf{Z}_1\otimes\operatorname{grad}\rho+\rho\operatorname{grad}\mathbf{Z}_1\in L^2$, and near $x^*$ the term $\rho\operatorname{grad}\mathbf{Z}_1$ tends to zero, so $[\![ \operatorname{grad}{\bf z}]\!]=\mathbf{Z}_1(x^*)\otimes[\![ \operatorname{grad}\rho]\!]$, whose trace is the jump of $\operatorname{div}{\bf z}$; ${\bf z}$ itself is continuous. For the last statement, $2\mathbf{Z}_1\cdot\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma=\chi\tilde j=j$ on $\Gamma$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- cor-11: body_md -->
\label{cor:hcurl_density}
For every value of the trainable parameters, every element of ${\mathcal{E}}_m^{\rm dR}$ lies in $H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$ and $\operatorname{curl}(\operatorname{grad}\phi_m+\mathcal Sr_m+\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}})=r_m$. For ${\bf E}$ as in \eqref{hcurl_representation}
\begin{equation}\label{hcurl_2d_approx}
 \inf_{r_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|r-r_m\|_0\le\inf_{{\bf E}_m\in{\mathcal{E}}_m^{\rm dR}}\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}
 \le\inf_{\phi_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\phi-\phi_m|_1+\sqrt{1+C_{x_0}^2}\,\inf_{r_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|r-r_m\|_0 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end cor-11 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Apply the isometry $K$ and Corollary~\ref{cor:hdiv23}. Directly: ${\bf E}-{\bf E}_m=\operatorname{grad}(\phi-\phi_m)+\mathcal S(r-r_m)$,
the first term is curl free, and $\|\mathcal
S(r-r_m)\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}^2=\|\mathcal{R}_2(r-r_m)\|_0^2+\|r-r_m\|_0^2\le(1+C_{x_0}^2)\|r-r_m\|_0^2$. The lower bound holds since $\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}_m=r_m$ gives $\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\ge\|r-r_m\|_0$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- cor-12: body_md -->
\label{cor:hcurl2d_reg}
Every ${\bf E}\in H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$ can be written ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\phi+{\bf z}$ with $\phi\in H^1(\Omega)$
and ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^2$ depending linearly on ${\bf E}$, $\|\phi\|_1+\|{\bf z}\|_1\le C\|{\bf E}\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}$ and $\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}=\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}$.
For every value of the trainable parameters, every
element of ${\mathcal{E}}_m^{\rm reg}$ lies in $H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$ and $\operatorname{curl}(\operatorname{grad}\phi_m+{\bf z}_m)=\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}_m$, and
\begin{equation}\label{hcurl_2d_reg_approx}
 \inf_{{\bf E}_m\in{\mathcal{E}}_m^{\rm reg}}\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\inf_{\phi_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\phi-\phi_m|_1+\sqrt2\inf_{{\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m}\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_1 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end cor-12 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Apply Proposition~\ref{prop:hdiv_regular} with $n=2$ to $K{\bf E}$: $K{\bf E}=\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi+{\bf w}$ with $\psi\in
H^1(\Omega)$ and ${\bf w}\in H^1(\Omega)^2$. Since $K^{-1}=J$ and $J\operatorname{\mathbf{curl}}\psi=JK\operatorname{grad}\psi=\operatorname{grad}\psi$ by
\eqref{hcurl_rotation}, ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\psi+J{\bf w}$; take $\phi=\psi$ and ${\bf z}=J{\bf w}$, so that $\|\phi\|_1+\|{\bf z}\|_1\le C\|K{\bf E}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}
=C\|{\bf E}\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}$, and $\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}=\operatorname{div}(KJ{\bf w})=\operatorname{div}{\bf w}=\operatorname{div} K{\bf E}=\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}$. For the classes, $\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{grad}\phi_m=0$, and for ${\bf w}\in H^1(\Omega)^2$, $|\operatorname{curl}{\bf w}|^2\le2(|\partial_1w_2|^2+|\partial_2w_1|^2)$, so $\|{\bf w}\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\sqrt2\|{\bf w}\|_1$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:hcurl_cpwl}
If $\phi$ is continuous and piecewise affine on a finite polygonal partition, then $\operatorname{grad}\phi$ is piecewise constant and belongs to $H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$, and across every interior edge with unit tangent ${\bf t}$ and normal ${\bf n}$
\begin{equation}\label{hcurl_cpwl_jump}
 [\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi\cdot{\bf t}]\!]=0,\qquad [\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi\cdot{\bf n}]\!]\ \mbox{may be nonzero}.
\end{equation}
This applies to every finite scalar ReLU network.
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The trace of $\phi$ on the edge is
single valued, so its tangential derivative is the
same from both sides; the piecewise curl of
$\operatorname{grad}\phi$ vanishes, and the continuity of the tangential trace
excludes an edge contribution to the distributional curl.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:hcurl_intrinsic}
If $\phi$ is continuous and piecewise affine on a polyhedral partition of
$\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^3$, then $\operatorname{grad}\phi$ is piecewise constant, $\operatorname{grad}\phi\in H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$, and
across every interior face $F$ with unit normal ${\bf n}_F$
\begin{equation}\label{hcurl_intrinsic_jump}
 {\bf n}_F\times[\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi]\!]=0,\qquad [\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi\cdot{\bf n}_F]\!]\ \mbox{may be nonzero}.
\end{equation}
Consequently $\operatorname{grad}\phi+{\bf z}$ with ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$ has the same tangential continuity and may retain the normal jump of $\operatorname{grad}\phi$.
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
The restriction of $\phi$ to $F$ is single valued,
so all tangential derivatives agree from both sides, which is ${\bf n}_F\times[\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi]\!]=0$;
the distributional curl therefore has no surface part on $F$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:hcurl3d_density}
Every realization in ${\mathcal{E}}_m$ lies in $H_{\Gamma_D}(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$, and for a regular decomposition ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\phi+{\bf z}$
\begin{equation}\label{hcurl_3d_approx}
 \inf_{{\bf E}_m\in{\mathcal{E}}_m}\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\inf_{\phi_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\phi-\phi_m|_1+\sqrt2\inf_{{\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m}\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_1 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
For ${\bf E}_m=\operatorname{grad}\phi_m+{\bf z}_m$ we have $\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}_m=\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}_m$, hence $\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le|\phi-\phi_m|_1+\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}$, and $\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}^2=\|{\bf v}\|_0^2+\|\operatorname{curl}{\bf v}\|_0^2\le\|{\bf v}\|_0^2+2|{\bf v}|_1^2\le2\|{\bf v}\|_1^2$ since $|\operatorname{curl}{\bf v}|^2\le2|\operatorname{grad}{\bf v}|^2$ pointwise; the regular decomposition is surjective, so every ${\bf E}$ has such a representation.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- rem-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:hcurl_regular}
If $\Gamma_D=\emptyset$ and ${\bf E},\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}\in H^r(\Omega)^3$, $r\ge0$, the regular decomposition can be
chosen with $\phi\in H^{r+1}(\Omega)$, ${\bf z}\in H^{r+1}(\Omega)^3$ and $\|\phi\|_{r+1}+\|{\bf z}\|_{r+1}\le C_r(\|{\bf E}\|_r+\|\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}\|_r)$: a field ${\bf z}_0\in
H^{r+1}(\Omega)^3$ with $\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}_0=\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}$ exists by \cite[Corollary~4.7(a)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010}, and the curl-free remainder ${\bf E}-{\bf z}_0$ is
$\operatorname{grad}\phi$ plus a smooth field of a finite-dimensional space
\cite[Theorem~4.9(b)]{CostabelMcIntosh2010}, which is added to ${\bf z}_0$. This is used in Proposition~\ref{prop:hcurl_recovery}.
<!-- end rem-6 -->
<!-- rem-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:rd_not_helmholtz}
\eqref{regular_decomposition} is not a Helmholtz decomposition: ${\bf z}$ is neither divergence free nor orthogonal to gradients. This matters for the neural parameterization, since on a nonconvex domain the divergence-free part of a Helmholtz
decomposition may lie in $H(\operatorname{curl})\cap H(\operatorname{div})$ without belonging to $H^1(\Omega)^3$ \cite{Cos:91}; the regular decomposition is used because both of its parts are in $H^1$.
<!-- end rem-7 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:hcurl_jump}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^n$, $n=2,3$, let $\Gamma$ be a smooth interface with unit normal ${\bf n}$, and let ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\phi+{\bf w}$ near $\Gamma$, where $\phi\in H^1$ is piecewise $H^2$ on the two sides of $\Gamma$, and ${\bf w}\in H(\operatorname{curl})$ has no jump across $\Gamma$ (its one-sided traces coincide; ${\bf w}\in H^1$ is sufficient). Then, with one-sided traces,
\[
 P_\tau[\![ {\bf E}]\!]=0,\qquad [\![ {\bf E}\cdot{\bf n}]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi]\!] ,
\]
and a prescribed normal jump $j$ is carried by the kink $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi]\!]=j$, which
it determines. For the modules the same formulas hold in the sense
of one-sided essential limits, under the weaker local assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:mult_kink}: for $\phi=\phi_0+|d_\Gamma|N$
with $\phi_0,N\in H^1$ and $d_\Gamma$ as in Section~\ref{subsec:interface}, $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi]\!]=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N$
at the points of $\Gamma$ near which $N$ is Lipschitz continuous
and $\phi_0$ is continuously differentiable; for $\phi=\phi_0+\operatorname{ReLU}(g_\eta)N$, $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi]\!]=N\operatorname{grad}
g_\eta$, a normal vector, at the regular points of $\Gamma_\eta$ (as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:learned_interface_hdiv}) near which $N$ is Lipschitz continuous and $\phi_0$ is
continuously differentiable. In both cases $\operatorname{grad}\phi+{\bf w}\in H(\operatorname{curl})$ for every value of the parameters.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
$\phi$ has a single-valued trace on $\Gamma$, so $[\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi]\!]{\bf n}$, and
$[\![ {\bf E}]\!]=[\![ \operatorname{grad}\phi]\!]$. The modules are treated as in Propositions~\ref{prop:interface_module} and~\ref{prop:learned_interface_hdiv} with the scalar $N$
in place of the skew matrix. Conformity holds because $\phi\in H^1$ and $\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{grad}\phi=0$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- cor-13: body_md -->
\label{cor:hcurl_jump_amplitude}
With $U$, $G$ and $C_{\rm tr}$ as in Corollary~\ref{cor:jump_amplitude}, $\|2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|(M-M')\|_{L^2(\Gamma)}\le2G\,C_{\rm tr}\|M-M'\|_{H^1(U)}$ for
$M,M'\in H^1(U)$. In particular, if $N$ is the amplitude of a jump $j$ and $N_m$ are Lipschitz networks, then $\|[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}(|d_\Gamma|N_m)]\!]-j\|_{L^2(\Gamma)}\le2G\,C_{\rm tr}\|N-N_m\|_{H^1(U)}$.
<!-- end cor-13 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Apply the trace inequality to $M-M'$; the identity $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}(|d_\Gamma|N_m)]\!]=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N_m$ is Proposition~\ref{prop:hcurl_jump} with $\phi_0=0$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:hcurl_recovery}
Let $n=2,3$, and let $\Gamma$, $\Omega_\pm$, $d_\Gamma$ and $U_\Gamma$ be as in Proposition~\ref{prop:regularity_recovery}. Let ${\bf E}\in H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$ with ${\bf E}|_{\Omega_\pm}\in H^1(\Omega_\pm)^n$ and normal jump $j:=[\![ {\bf E}\cdot{\bf n}]\!]$ on $\Gamma$, and let $N\in H^2(\Omega)$ be supported in $U_\Gamma$ with $2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N=j$ on $\Gamma$. Then
\[
 {\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\bigl(|d_\Gamma|N\bigr)+{\bf z},\qquad {\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^n .
\]
This is the form of the second representation;
for $n=2$, in the first representation of \eqref{hcurl_representation}, ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}(\phi_0+|d_\Gamma|N)+\mathcal
Sr+\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ with $\phi_0\in H^1(\Omega)$, $r=\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}$ and $\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ the cohomology part of ${\bf E}$. If
$n=3$ and $\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$, then ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}(\phi_0+|d_\Gamma|N)+{\bf z}'$ with $\phi_0\in H^2(\Omega)$ and ${\bf z}'\in H^2(\Omega)^3$.
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Since ${\bf E}\in H(\operatorname{curl};\Omega)$, its tangential trace is single valued, $P_\tau[\![ {\bf E}]\!]=0$, so $[\![ {\bf E}]\!]=j{\bf n}$. On $\Omega_\pm$, $|d_\Gamma|N=\pm d_\Gamma N\in H^2$, so $\operatorname{grad}(|d_\Gamma|N)=N\operatorname{grad}|d_\Gamma|+|d_\Gamma|\operatorname{grad} N$ is in $H^1$ on both sides, with traces $\pm N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma$ on $\Gamma$; its jump is $2N\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma=2|\operatorname{grad} d_\Gamma|N{\bf n}=j{\bf n}$. Hence ${\bf z}:={\bf E}-\operatorname{grad}(|d_\Gamma|N)$ is in $H^1$ on both sides and has no jump across $\Gamma$, so ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^n$ by the same gluing argument. For $n=2$, \eqref{hcurl_representation} applied to ${\bf z}$ gives ${\bf z}=\operatorname{grad}\phi_0+\mathcal Sr+\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ with $r=\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}=\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}$, and $\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ has the circulations of ${\bf z}$, which are those of ${\bf E}$, since the gradient of an $H^1$ function has zero circulations. For $n=3$, $\operatorname{curl}{\bf z}=\operatorname{curl}{\bf E}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$, and Remark~\ref{rem:hcurl_regular} with $r=1$ gives ${\bf z}=\operatorname{grad}\phi_0+{\bf z}'$ with $\phi_0\in H^2(\Omega)$ and ${\bf z}'\in H^2(\Omega)^3$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- cor-14: body_md -->
\label{cor:enriched_hcurl}
Let $d_\Gamma\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$, let $\{{\mathcal{P}}_m'\}$ be a second family of scalar
classes, and let $C_d$ be as in Corollary~\ref{cor:enriched_hdiv}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] For $n=2$, ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}(\phi_0+|d_\Gamma|N)+\mathcal Sr+\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ with $\phi_0,N\in H^1(\Omega)$, $r\in L^2(\Omega)$, $\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}\in J{\mathcal{H}}$, and the class of
the fields $\operatorname{grad}(\phi_{0,m}+|d_\Gamma|N_m)+\mathcal Sr_m+\mathbf{h}_{\operatorname{curl}}$ with $\phi_{0,m}\in{\mathcal{P}}_m$, $N_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m'$, $r_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m$,
\[
 \inf_{{\bf E}_m}\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\inf_{\phi_{0,m}\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\phi_0-\phi_{0,m}|_1+C_d\inf_{N_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m'}\|N-N_m\|_1+\sqrt{1+C_{x_0}^2}\,\inf_{r_m\in{\mathcal{Q}}_m}\|r-r_m\|_0 .
\]
\item[(b)] For $n=3$ and $\Gamma_D=\emptyset$, ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}(\phi_0+|d_\Gamma|N)+{\bf z}$ with $\phi_0,N\in H^1(\Omega)$, ${\bf z}\in H^1(\Omega)^3$, and the class of the fields
$\operatorname{grad}(\phi_{0,m}+|d_\Gamma|N_m)+{\bf z}_m$ with $\phi_{0,m}\in{\mathcal{P}}_m$, $N_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m'$, ${\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m$,
\[
 \inf_{{\bf E}_m}\|{\bf E}-{\bf E}_m\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\inf_{\phi_{0,m}\in{\mathcal{P}}_m}|\phi_0-\phi_{0,m}|_1+C_d\inf_{N_m\in{\mathcal{P}}_m'}\|N-N_m\|_1+\sqrt2\inf_{{\bf z}_m\in\mathcal{Z}_m}\|{\bf z}-{\bf z}_m\|_1 .
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end cor-14 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
In both cases ${\bf E}-{\bf E}_m$ is $\operatorname{grad}(\phi_0-\phi_{0,m})+\operatorname{grad}(|d_\Gamma|(N-N_m))$ plus $\mathcal S(r-r_m)$ for $n=2$ and ${\bf z}-{\bf z}_m$ for $n=3$. The gradients are curl free, and $|\,|d_\Gamma|M|_1\le C_d\|M\|_1$ as in Corollary~\ref{cor:enriched_hdiv}; $\|\mathcal S(r-r_m)\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le(1+C_{x_0}^2)^{1/2}\|r-r_m\|_0$ as in the proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:hcurl_density}, and $\|{\bf v}\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}\le\sqrt2\|{\bf v}\|_1$ as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:hcurl3d_density}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- rem-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:kink_intrinsic}
The regular decomposition is not unique, but its singular part
is. If ${\bf E}=\operatorname{grad}\phi_1+{\bf z}_1=\operatorname{grad}\phi_2+{\bf z}_2$ with ${\bf z}_1,{\bf z}_2\in H^1(\Omega)^n$, then $\operatorname{grad}(\phi_1-\phi_2)={\bf z}_2-{\bf z}_1\in H^1(\Omega)^n$, so $\phi_1-\phi_2\in
H^2(\Omega)$; where one-sided traces exist, $[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi_1]\!]=[\![ \partial_{{\bf n}}\phi_2]\!]=[\![ {\bf E}\cdot{\bf n}]\!]$. The kink is
determined by the field, not by the decomposition operator.
<!-- end rem-8 -->
<!-- rem-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:nedelec}
In two dimensions $K^{-1}=J$ maps the Raviart--Thomas spaces
onto the N\'ed\'elec spaces of the first kind and curls
to gradients; Remark~\ref{rem:RT0} therefore gives, on a triangle
$T$ with a local center $x_T$ and $\mathcal S_T:=J\mathcal{R}_{2,T}$,
\[
 N_k(T)=J\,RT_k(T)=\operatorname{grad}\mathbb P_{k+1}\oplus\mathcal S_T\mathbb P_k ,
\]
so $\operatorname{grad}\phi+\mathcal S_Tr$ with $\phi\in\mathbb P_{k+1}$ and $r\in\mathbb P_k$ is exactly $N_k(T)$, and the class ${\mathcal{E}}_m^{\rm dR}$ of \eqref{X_hcurl_2d} replaces the polynomials $\phi$ and $r$ by networks. On a conforming triangulation $\mathcal T_h$ of a simply connected
$\Omega$, the curl-free fields of the lowest-order N\'ed\'elec space are $\operatorname{grad} S_1(\mathcal T_h)$, and every function in $S_1(\mathcal T_h)$ is a ReLU network \cite{HLXZ:20}; this is the rotation of Remark~\ref{rem:RT0_2d}. In three dimensions
$N_k(T)=\mathbb P_k^3\oplus(x-x_T)\times\widetilde{\mathbb P}_k^3=\operatorname{grad}\mathbb P_{k+1}\oplus(x-x_T)\times\mathbb P_k^3$ \cite{AFW:2006}, where the second part is the Poincar\'e operator applied to the curl. The class \eqref{structured3d} replaces it by the $H^1$ field ${\bf z}$, since the curl cannot be prescribed freely (Section~\ref{subsec:hcurl_derham}), and no correspondence with N\'ed\'elec spaces is claimed.
<!-- end rem-9 -->
<!-- assum-1: body_md -->
\label{assum:fosls}
There are $0<c_L\le C_L$ with $c_L\|z\|_X^2\le\|Lz\|_Y^2\le C_L\|z\|_X^2$ for
all $z\in X$, and $Lz^*=F$ has a solution $z^*\in X$.
<!-- end assum-1 -->
<!-- defn-1: body_md -->
\label{def:nativefosls}
For $j=1,\dots,J$ let $\{\mathcal{X}_{j,m}\}_m$ be nested classes for $X_j$: ordinary networks for scalar unknowns in $H^1$ or $L^2$, and the native classes of Sections~\ref{sec:hdiv}--\ref{sec:hcurl} for vector unknowns (for $H(\operatorname{div})$ either the class of Section~\ref{sec:hdiv} or that of Section~\ref{sec:hdiv_reg}, for $H(\operatorname{curl})$ in two dimensions either class of Section~\ref{subsec:hcurl2d}). Every realization lies in $X_j$ for every parameter value. Let $\mathcal{X}_m:=\mathcal{X}_{1,m}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{J,m}$. A FOSLS-deRhaNN approximation is any $z_m\in\mathcal{X}_m$ with ${\mathcal{J}}(z_m;F)\le\inf_{\mathcal{X}_m}{\mathcal{J}}(\cdot;F)+\varepsilon_m^2$, where $\varepsilon_m\ge0$ is
a suboptimality defect (from nonlinear optimization, stopping, regularized linear algebra or another numerical effect, which are not distinguished here). We call the resulting method \emph{FOSLS-deRhaNN}, first-order system least squares with de Rham neural networks, in which the graph-space unknowns are represented by the deRhaNN classes developed above and the loss is the FOSLS functional posed in the natural spaces of the weak formulation.
<!-- end defn-1 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:best_approx}
Under Assumption~\ref{assum:fosls}, $\mathcal{X}_m\subset X$ for every value of the trainable parameters, and
\[
 c_L\|z^*-z_m\|_X^2\le{\mathcal{J}}(z_m;F)\le C_L\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}_m}\|z^*-x\|_X^2+\varepsilon_m^2 .
\]
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
For every $x\in\mathcal{X}_m$, ${\mathcal{J}}(z_m;F)\le{\mathcal{J}}(x;F)+\varepsilon_m^2=\|L(x-z^*)\|_Y^2+\varepsilon_m^2\le C_L\|x-z^*\|_X^2+\varepsilon_m^2$, and ${\mathcal{J}}(z_m;F)=\|L(z_m-z^*)\|_Y^2\ge c_L\|z_m-z^*\|_X^2$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- rem-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:shared}
The approximation estimates of Sections~\ref{sec:hdiv}--\ref{sec:hcurl} minimize over each representing function independently, that is, over a product of scalar classes. In the computations the representing functions are outputs of
one network with shared hidden layers, which at a fixed width is in general a proper subset of that product. Conversely, $s$ networks of the
same depth with widths $m_1,\ldots,m_s$ form one network with $s$ outputs and width $m_1+\cdots+m_s$: the first hidden layers are stacked, the deeper ones are block diagonal, and the output layer selects
the blocks. The estimates over the product therefore hold for the shared architecture with the widths added, not at the same width.
<!-- end rem-10 -->
<!-- rem-11: body_md -->
\label{rem:variants}
\emph{Boundary conditions.} Inhomogeneous essential conditions are imposed exactly in the classes (affine sets) or weakly through
the functional (Section~\ref{subsec:weak_dirichlet}). \emph{Built-in equations.} If $L=(L_1,L_2)$ and the trial set is restricted to $\{L_2z=F_2\}$, which $z^*$ satisfies,
then $L_2(z-z^*)=0$, so ${\mathcal{J}}=\|L_1z-F_1\|^2$ on this set and the stability of Assumption~\ref{assum:fosls} restricts to it; Proposition~\ref{prop:best_approx}
holds unchanged. Any $z_{2,p}$ with $L_2z_{2,p}=F_2$ gives the affine representation $z_{2,p}+\ker L_2$, with native classes
for $\ker L_2$; a bounded right inverse $R_2$ of $L_2$ is a
convenient stable construction of such a lift, $z_{2,p}=R_2F_2$, and is not needed for the stability. \emph{Density.}
Conformity alone does not suffice: if the closure of the union of the classes is
a proper subspace of $X$, the best approximation error stays bounded away from zero. With Maxwell-type
boundary conditions on nonsmooth domains, an $H^1$-conforming single-field class may fail to be
dense in the corresponding intersection graph space \cite{Cos:91}; this is avoided by the first-order formulations used here.
<!-- end rem-11 -->
<!-- rem-12: body_md -->
\label{rem:uniform}
As in least-squares finite element methods, the first-order system is chosen so that
every variable lies in one continuity space and each continuity space has its own
variable: $u\in H^1$ and $\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\in H(\operatorname{div})$ for second-order equations, ${\bf E}\in H(\operatorname{curl})$ and ${\bf D}\in
H(\operatorname{div})$ for div--curl systems. The native classes are taken for each variable separately.
What remains problem-specific is the equivalence of Assumption~\ref{assum:fosls} for the chosen
system, as for least-squares finite elements. An exterior-derivative
equation $d\omega=g$ can be built into the trial class with
$g$ an unconstrained trainable function only in top degree: in intermediate degree right
inverses exist, but $g$ must satisfy $dg=0$, which an arbitrary network does not satisfy automatically.
<!-- end rem-12 -->
<!-- rem-13: body_md -->
\label{rem:div_levels}
Suppose the first-order system contains an equation $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+B(z')=f$ for the divergence of its $H(\operatorname{div})$ unknown, with $B$ acting on
the other unknowns $z'$; in Section~\ref{sec:method} $B(v)={\bf b}\cdot\operatorname{grad} v+cv$, in Section~\ref{sec:transport} $B(v)=\gamma v$. \emph{(i) Simplification.} In the
first class $\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=\operatorname{Div}A+\mathcal{R}_nq+\mathbf{h}$ the divergence is one of the representing functions, $\operatorname{div}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}=q$ exactly, so the divergence
residual is $q+B(z')-f$: it contains $q$ by its values and no derivative of the potential, which
enters only the other residuals. In the second class the divergence residual is $\operatorname{div}{\bf z}+B(z')-f$: it contains the first derivatives of
${\bf z}$ and, again, no derivative of the potential. This is used in the formulations of Sections~\ref{sec:method},
\ref{sec:transport} and~\ref{sec:conslaw}, which contain a divergence equation, and in their numerical examples, except the generic level of Section~\ref{subsec:tr_numerics} with
the first class. The curl--curl system of Section~\ref{sec:curlcurl} has no divergence equation. \emph{(ii) Elimination.} With the first
class (Section~\ref{sec:hdiv}), for some problems the divergence $q$ can be eliminated by $q:=f-B(z')$, which builds the divergence equation into the
class with the lift $\mathcal{R}_n(f-B(z'))$ (the built-in equations of Remark~\ref{rem:variants}); the divergence residual then vanishes identically. This removes one network and one
residual, at the price of tying $q$ to $z'$ (for the elliptic equation through $\operatorname{grad} v$, Section~\ref{subsec:balanced}). It is
an option, not the main formulation; it is used for the generic level of transport with the first class (Section~\ref{subsec:tr_numerics}).
<!-- end rem-13 -->
<!-- assum-2: body_md -->
\label{assum:main}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^2$ is a bounded Lipschitz polygon.
$\Omega$ is partitioned into polygonal subdomains $\{\Omega_i\}_{i=1}^m$ (the coefficient subdomains).
\item[(ii)] $\mathbf A\in L^\infty(\Omega)^{2\times2}$ is symmetric with $\lambda_0|\xi|^2\le\xi^T\mathbf A\xi\le\lambda_1|\xi|^2$; ${\bf b},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in L^\infty(\Omega)^2$; $c\in L^\infty(\Omega)$.
No derivatives of the coefficients are used.
\item[(iii)] The data are those of the weak formulation: $f_1\in L^2(\Omega)$, ${\bf f}_2\in L^2(\Omega)^2$,
and $g_D\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)$.
\item[(iv)] The bilinear form
$a(v,w):=(\mathbf A\operatorname{grad} v-\boldsymbol{\beta} v,\operatorname{grad} w)+({\bf b}\cdot\operatorname{grad} v+cv,w)$ induces an isomorphism $H^1_0(\Omega)\to H^{-1}(\Omega)$. Equivalently, the homogeneous
problem has only the trivial solution: $a$ satisfies a G\aa rding inequality, and the lower-order terms are compact from $H^1_0(\Omega)$
to $H^{-1}(\Omega)$ ($v\mapsto{\bf b}\cdot\operatorname{grad} v+cv$ is bounded into $L^2(\Omega)$, which embeds compactly into $H^{-1}(\Omega)$, and
$v\mapsto\boldsymbol{\beta} v$ is compact into $L^2(\Omega)^2$ by Rellich's theorem), so the operator is Fredholm of index zero.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end assum-2 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:equiv}
Under Assumption~\ref{assum:main} there are constants $0<C_\ell\le C_u$ such that $$ C_\ell\big(\|v\|_1^2+\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}^2\big)\le|\!|\!|(v,\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})|\!|\!|^2\le C_u\big(\|v\|_1^2+\|\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}^2\big)\qquad\forall\,(v,\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})\in H^1_0(\Omega)\times H(\operatorname{div};\Omega). $$
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- rem-14: body_md -->
\label{rem:Hminus1}
Lemma~\ref{lem:equiv} concerns the homogeneous operator only, so the error identity and the norm equivalence hold for all data in Assumption~\ref{assum:main}(iii), with no regularity beyond
that of the weak formulation \cite{Zhang:23}. In the native classes of Section~\ref{sec:hdiv} only $f_1$ enters the right inverse (through $q=f_1-{\bf b}\cdot\operatorname{grad}
v-cv$ in the balanced formulation below), in those of Section~\ref{sec:hdiv_reg} it enters only the divergence residual, and in both ${\bf f}_2$ enters only the constitutive residual, pointwise
and never differentiated. The representation $F=f_1+\operatorname{div}{\bf f}_2$ of a datum in $H^{-1}(\Omega)$ is not unique: the weak solution $u$ does not depend on it, but the flux $\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=-\mathbf A\operatorname{grad} u+\boldsymbol{\beta}
u-{\bf f}_2$ does, and with it the regularity, the interface structure and the best approximation of the flux. The representation
is therefore part of the first-order formulation; when the data come with a natural one, such as a source on an interface
or a curve, or a given physical flux, that one is used. A strong-form residual $-\operatorname{div}(\mathbf A\operatorname{grad} v)+\cdots-f_1-\operatorname{div}{\bf f}_2$ is not defined
for such data. The Dirichlet data are imposed either in the trial set, $v\in H^1_g(\Omega)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:qo}), or weakly through the functional (Section~\ref{subsec:weak_dirichlet}).
<!-- end rem-14 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:qo}
Suppose ${\mathcal{U}}_g\subset H^1_g(\Omega)$. Then
\[
{\mathtt{LS}}(u_\theta,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_\theta;F)
=|\!|\!|(u-u_\theta,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_\theta)|\!|\!|^2
\le
\inf_{(v,\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})\in{\mathcal{U}}_g\times\Sigma_\theta}
|\!|\!|(u-v,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})|\!|\!|^2+\varepsilon^2,
\]
and consequently
\[
\|u-u_\theta\|_1^2+\|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_\theta\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}^2
\le\frac{C_u}{C_\ell}
\inf_{(v,\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$})\in{\mathcal{U}}_g\times\Sigma_\theta}
\big(\|u-v\|_1^2+\|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}^2\big)
+\frac{\varepsilon^2}{C_\ell}.
\]
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
